is called the Lindbladian.

\hat{H}
is the Hamiltonian of the system which determines the coherent dynamics.
The dissipator,

{\mathcal{D}}[\hat{L}]\hat{\rho}=\hat{L}\hat{\rho}\hat{L}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{L}^{\dagger}\hat{L},\hat{\rho}\}
,
is the term that makes the time evolution non-unitary.
The so-called jump operator
\hat{L}_{\ell}
consists of operators acting on the system, which
describes how the environment interacts with the system with the dissipation rate
\kappa_{\ell}

in channel
\ell
.
For instance,
suppose the system consists of bosonic/fermionic particles and
one takes
\hat{L}_{\ell}=\hat{a}
(where
\hat{a}
is an annihilation operator of a particle in the system) and
\kappa_{\ell}=\kappa
.
This corresponds to the case where the environment induces one-body loss from the system with the loss rate
\kappa
.
When one takes
\hat{L}=\hat{a}^{\dagger}
, on the other hand, it describes the situation where the particles are pumped into the system.
Taking
\hat{L}=\hat{a}^{2}
means two-body loss, etc.

I.1.1
General properties of the GKSL master equation

Here we review some general properties of the GKSL master equation (
S4
) relevant to our study.
To formulate them, it is useful to express the GKSL master equation in terms of the vectorized form to make explicit connections to the more familiar Schrödinger equation.
The vectorization (Choi-Jamiolkowski isomorphism) is chosen to be performed by the replacement
[
60
]
,

\displaystyle\lvert i\rangle\langle j\rvert\rightarrow\lvert j\rangle\otimes\lvert i\rangle.

(S5)

For example, an operator
\hat{A}=\sum_{i,j}a_{i,j}\lvert i\rangle\langle j\rvert
is vectorized as

\displaystyle{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho})=\sum_{i,j}a_{i,j}\lvert j\rangle\otimes\lvert i\rangle.

(S6)

Their dual

\hat{A}^{\dagger}=\sum_{i,j}a_{i,j}^{*}\lvert j\rangle\langle i\rvert

is vectorized as

\displaystyle[{\rm vec}(\hat{A})]^{\dagger}=\sum_{i,j}a_{i,j}^{*}\langle j\rvert\otimes\langle i\rvert.

(S7)

This choice of vectorization is useful, thanks to the following properties.
First, the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product
(\hat{A},\hat{B})={\rm{tr}}[{\hat{A}^{\dagger}\hat{B}}]
can be expressed as

\displaystyle(\hat{A},\hat{B})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm{tr}}[{\hat{A}^{\dagger}\hat{B}}]=\sum_{i,j,i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}a_{i,j}^{*}b_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}{\rm tr}[(\lvert i\rangle\langle j\rvert)^{\dagger}(\lvert i^{\prime}\rangle\langle j^{\prime}\rvert)]=\sum_{i,j}a_{i,j}^{*}b_{i,j}

(S8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j,i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}(a_{j,i}^{*}\langle i\rvert\otimes\langle j\rvert)(b_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\lvert j^{\prime}\rangle\otimes\lvert i^{\prime}\rangle)=[{\rm vec}(\hat{A})]^{\dagger}{\rm vec}(\hat{B})

which is just the inner product of the two vectors.
Another useful property is the relation

\displaystyle{\rm vec}(\hat{A}\hat{B}\hat{C})=(\hat{C}^{\mathrm{T}}\otimes\hat{A}){\rm vec}({\hat{B}}),

(S9)

which can be shown similarly by decomposing the matrix in terms of the chosen eigenbasis.
This relation implies (where
\hat{I}=\sum_{i}\lvert i\rangle\otimes\langle i\rvert
is the identity operator)

\displaystyle{\rm vec}(\hat{A}\hat{\rho}\hat{B})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\hat{B}^{\mathrm{T}}\otimes\hat{A}){\rm vec}(\hat{\rho})

(S10)

\displaystyle{\rm vec}(\hat{A}\hat{\rho})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm vec}(\hat{A}\hat{\rho}\hat{I})=(\hat{I}\otimes\hat{A}){\rm vec}(\hat{\rho})

(S11)

\displaystyle{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho}\hat{A})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm vec}(\hat{I}\hat{\rho}\hat{A})=(\hat{A}^{\mathrm{T}}\otimes\hat{I}){\rm vec}(\hat{\rho}),

(S12)

allowing us to express the GKSL master equation (
S4
) in the vectorized form,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rm{vec}(\hat{\rho})=\breve{\mathcal{L}}\rm{vec}(\hat{\rho}),

(S13)

where

\displaystyle\breve{\mathcal{L}}=-i(\hat{I}\otimes\hat{H}-\hat{H}^{\mathrm{T}}\otimes\hat{I})+\sum_{\ell}\kappa_{\ell}\Big[\hat{L}_{\ell}^{*}\otimes\hat{L}_{\ell}-\frac{1}{2}\hat{I}\otimes\hat{L}_{\ell}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{\ell}-\frac{1}{2}(\hat{L}_{\ell}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{\ell})^{\mathrm{T}}\otimes\hat{I}\Big].

(S14)

Equation (
S13
) formally has a similar form to the Schrödinger equation of quantum mechanics.
However, unlike in the Schrödinger equation, the operator
\check{\mathcal{L}}
that governs the dynamics is non-Hermitian.
In those cases, it is useful to characterize the
\check{\mathcal{L}}
with
both
the right and left eigenvectors (
\hat{r}_{n}
and
\hat{l}_{n}
, respectively),

\displaystyle\breve{\mathcal{L}}{\rm{vec}}(\hat{r}_{n})=\lambda_{n}{\rm{vec}}(\hat{r}_{n}),\qquad\breve{\mathcal{L}}^{\dagger}{\rm{vec}}(\hat{l}_{n})=\lambda_{n}^{*}{\rm{vec}}(\hat{l}_{n}),

(S15)

where the eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}
are sorted as
\lambda_{N-1}\leq\lambda_{N-2}\leq...\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{0}
.
Note that in Hermitian cases,
\hat{r}_{n}=\hat{l}_{n}
but this does not generally hold otherwise.
In the operator form, these read

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}\hat{r}_{n}=\lambda_{n}\hat{r}_{n},\qquad\hat{l}_{n}^{\dagger}{\mathcal{L}}=\hat{l}_{n}^{\dagger}\lambda_{n},

(S16)

which form a biorthogonal and complete basis,

\displaystyle{\rm vec}(\hat{l}_{n})^{\dagger}{\rm vec}(\hat{r}_{m})=\delta_{nm},\qquad\sum_{n}{\rm vec}(\hat{r}_{n}){\rm vec}(\hat{l}_{n})^{\dagger}=\hat{I}.

(S17)

Here, we used the property that an inner product is properly defined (Eq. (
S8
)).
One can then decompose the density operator into eigenmodes of
\breve{\mathcal{L}}
:

\displaystyle{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho}(t))=e^{\breve{\mathcal{L}}t}{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho}(0))=\sum_{n}e^{\lambda_{n}t}{\rm vec}(\hat{r}_{n}){\rm vec}(\hat{l}_{n})^{\dagger}\hat{\rho}(0),

(S18)

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(t)=e^{{\mathcal{L}}t}\hat{\rho}(0)=\sum_{n}e^{\lambda_{n}t}\hat{r}_{n}{\rm tr}[\hat{l}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{\rho}(0)].

(S19)

As one sees from here,
-{\rm Re}\lambda_{n}
characterizes the decay rate of the mode
n
and
{\rm Im}\lambda_{n}
characterizes its oscillation frequency.

We remark that the GKSL master equation (
S4
) is guaranteed to have at least one eigenstate with a vanishing eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}=0
, corresponding to the steady state
\partial_{t}\hat{\rho}_{\rm ss}=0
.
This can be shown from the trace-preserving property of the GKSL master equation that the dynamics assure
{\rm tr}\hat{\rho}(t)=1
at arbitrary times,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\rm tr}\hat{\rho}={\rm tr}[{\mathcal{L}}\hat{\rho}]=0.

(S20)

In the vectorized GKSL master equation, this reads

\displaystyle 0={\rm vec}(\hat{I})^{\dagger}\breve{\mathcal{L}}{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho}).

(S21)

Here, we have used the relation
{\rm tr}(\hat{A})={\rm vec}(\hat{I})^{\dagger}{\rm vec}(\hat{A})
where
{\rm vec}(\hat{I})=\sum_{i}\lvert i\rangle\otimes\lvert i\rangle
.
For this to be true for arbitrary
\hat{\rho}
,

\displaystyle 0={\rm vec}(I)^{\dagger}\breve{\mathcal{L}}

(S22)

must be satisfied.
This assures the presence of a left eigenstate given by

\displaystyle\hat{l}_{0}=\hat{I},

(S23)

with zero eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}=0
.
The right eigenstate is the state satisfying

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\hat{r}_{0})=0,

(S24)

which is nothing but a steady state
\hat{r}_{0}=\hat{\rho}_{ss}
.
Using this property, we find

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(t)=\hat{\rho}_{ss}+\sum_{n\neq 0}e^{\lambda_{n}t}\hat{r}_{n}{\rm tr}[\hat{l}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{\rho}(0)].

(S25)

Here, the coefficient in front of
\hat{\rho}_{ss}
is unity
{\rm tr}[\hat{l}_{0}^{\dagger}\hat{\rho}(0)]=1
, which can be shown from orthogonality relation (
S17
) that implies
{\rm tr}[\hat{l}_{m}\hat{r}_{n}]=\delta_{n,m}

and
\hat{l}_{0}=\hat{I}
.
One also finds that the
n\neq 0
right eigenstates are traceless, from
{\rm tr}[\hat{r}_{n}]={\rm tr}[\hat{l}_{0}^{\dagger}\hat{r}_{n}]=0~(n\neq 0)
.

I.1.2
Projection method

There are many situations in condensed matter physics and quantum optics where there is a separation of timescales in the problem.
In such cases, it is often useful to project out the higher-energy degrees of freedom to obtain an effective low-energy description
[
52
,
61
,
62
]
.
These approaches have been proven useful in describing, e.g., electrons in a potential perturbed by impurities can be described by an effective
k\cdot p
theory
[
103
]
; the Fermi-Hubbard model at the half-filling can be mapped to an anti-ferromagnetically interacting spin model
[
104
]
; the Anderson impurity model can be mapped to an sd exchange coupling model
[
52
]
that has successfully described the Kondo effect
[
51
]
.

Here, we extend this concept to an open quantum system, which will be used in Sec.
III
.
We develop here a projection method that is consistent with the so-called Lindblad perturbation theory
[
63
,
64
,
65
]
, which is similar to the generalized Schrieffer-Wolff transformation technique
[
105
,
106
,
107
]
in spirit.
We consider a Lindbladian that can be split into the non-perturbative and perturbative part
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}_{0}+\epsilon{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
(where
\epsilon=1
is a book-keeping constant).
The non-perturbative Lindbladian master equation,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\rm{vec}}(\hat{\rho}^{(0)})=\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\rm{vec}(\hat{\rho}^{(0)})

(S26)

is assumed to have a separation of timescales.
We label a set of eigenvectors
\hat{r}_{n}^{(0)}[n\in\mathfrak{s(f)}]
with small [in magnitude] eigenvalues
|\lambda_{n\in\mathfrak{s}}^{(0)}|(<|\lambda_{n\in\mathfrak{f}}^{(0)}|)
as slow (fast) modes,
where

\hat{r}_{n}^{(0)}(\hat{l}_{n}^{(0)})
is the right (left) eigenstate of the non-perturbative Lindbladian defined as
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\hat{r}_{n}^{(0)}=\lambda_{n}^{(0)}\hat{r}_{n}^{(0)}

(
\hat{l}_{n}^{(0)\dagger}{\mathcal{L}}_{0}=\hat{l}_{n}^{(0)\dagger}\lambda_{n}^{(0)}
) with the eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}^{(0)}
.
We split the density operator into slow and fast part
\hat{\rho}^{(0)}=\hat{\rho}_{s}^{(0)}+\hat{\rho}_{f}^{(0)}
.
Here,
\hat{\rho}_{s}^{(0)}={\mathcal{P}}\hat{\rho}^{(0)}=\sum_{n\in\mathfrak{s}}c_{n}\hat{r}_{n}^{(0)}

is the density operator spanned projected to the slow variable space
(where
{\mathcal{P}}\hat{\rho}=\sum_{n\in\mathfrak{s}}\hat{r}_{n}^{(0)}{\rm tr}[\hat{l}_{n}^{(0)\dagger}\hat{\rho}]
is a projection operator onto the slow space)
and

\hat{\rho}_{f}^{(0)}={\mathcal{Q}}\hat{\rho}^{(0)}=({\mathcal{I}}-{\mathcal{P}})\hat{\rho}^{(0)}=\sum_{n\in\mathfrak{f}}c_{n}\hat{r}_{n}^{(0)}
is the fast variable space
(where

{\mathcal{Q}}\hat{\rho}=\sum_{n\in\mathfrak{f}}\hat{r}_{n}^{(0)}{\rm tr}[\hat{l}_{n}^{(0)\dagger}\hat{\rho}]
is a projection operator onto the fast variable space
and
{\mathcal{I}}
is an identity superoperator).
The non-perturbative GKSL master equation
\partial_{t}\hat{\rho}_{s}^{(0)}+\partial_{t}\hat{\rho}_{f}^{(0)}={\mathcal{L}}_{0}\hat{\rho}_{s}^{(0)}+{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\hat{\rho}_{f}^{(0)}
reads

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\breve{\mathcal{P}}{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho}^{(0)})\\
\breve{\mathcal{Q}}{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho}^{(0)})\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\breve{\mathcal{P}}\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{0}&0\\
0&\breve{\mathcal{Q}}\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\breve{\mathcal{P}}{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho}^{(0)})\\
\breve{\mathcal{Q}}{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho}^{(0)})\end{pmatrix}

(S27)

in the vectorized representation, where we used the property that these two spaces do not talk to each other via
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
.
Here, we have introduced a projection operator in the vectorized representation,
{\rm vec}({\mathcal{P}}\hat{\rho})\equiv\breve{\mathcal{P}}{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho})
and similarly for
\breve{\mathcal{Q}}
.

Let us now add back the perturbation
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
. In such a case, we find

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\begin{pmatrix}\breve{\mathcal{P}}{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho})\\
\breve{\mathcal{Q}}{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{t}\breve{\mathcal{P}}{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho})\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{ss}&\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{sf}\\
\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{fs}&\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{ff}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\breve{\mathcal{P}}{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho})\\
\breve{\mathcal{Q}}{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho})\end{pmatrix}

(S28)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\breve{\mathcal{P}}\breve{\mathcal{L}}&\epsilon\breve{\mathcal{P}}\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\\
\epsilon\breve{\mathcal{Q}}\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{1}&\breve{\mathcal{Q}}\breve{\mathcal{L}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\breve{\mathcal{P}}{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho})\\
\breve{\mathcal{Q}}{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho})\end{pmatrix}

where in the second equality, we have done an adiabatic approximation for the fast modes (i.e., the fast modes relax fast enough such that the fast variables adiabatically follow the slow variables).
This leads to

\displaystyle\breve{\mathcal{Q}}{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho})\approx-(\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{ff})^{-1}\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{fs}\breve{\mathcal{P}}{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho}),

(S29)

therefore,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\breve{\mathcal{P}}{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho})=\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm eff}\breve{\mathcal{P}}{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho})=[\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{ss}-\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{sf}(\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{ff})^{-1}\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{fs}]\breve{\mathcal{P}}{\rm vec}(\hat{\rho})

(S30)

or

\displaystyle\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm eff}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\breve{\mathcal{P}}\breve{\mathcal{L}}\breve{\mathcal{P}}-\epsilon^{2}\breve{\mathcal{P}}\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\breve{\mathcal{Q}}(\breve{\mathcal{L}})^{-1}\breve{\mathcal{Q}}\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\breve{\mathcal{P}}

(S31)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\breve{\mathcal{P}}\bigg[\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{0}+\epsilon\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{1}-\epsilon^{2}\sum_{m\in\mathfrak{f}}\frac{\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{1}{\rm vec}(\hat{r}_{m}^{(0)}){\rm vec}(\hat{l}_{m}^{(0)})^{\dagger}\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{1}}{\lambda_{m}^{(0)}}\bigg]\breve{\mathcal{P}}+O(\epsilon^{3})

The matrix element of the effective Lindbladian
({\mathcal{L}}_{\rm eff})_{n_{l}\in\mathfrak{s},n_{r}\in\mathfrak{s}}\equiv(\hat{l}_{n_{l}\in\mathfrak{s}}^{(0)},{\mathcal{L}_{\rm eff}\hat{r}_{n_{r}\in\mathfrak{s}}^{(0)}})
, which contains all the information about the system in the slow dynamics of interest,
reads

\displaystyle({\mathcal{L}}_{\rm eff})_{n_{l},n_{r}}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle(\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)},{\mathcal{L}_{\rm eff}\hat{r}_{n_{r}}^{(0)}})={\rm vec}(\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)})^{\dagger}\breve{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm eff}{\rm vec}(\hat{r}_{n_{r}}^{(0)})

(S32)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\mathcal{L}}_{0})_{n_{l},n_{r}}+\epsilon({\mathcal{L}}_{1})_{n_{l},n_{r}}-\epsilon^{2}\sum_{m\in\mathfrak{f}}\frac{{\rm tr}[\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)\dagger}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\hat{r}_{m}^{(0)}]{\rm tr}[\hat{l}_{m}^{(0)\dagger}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\hat{r}_{n_{r}}^{(0)}]}{\lambda_{m}^{(0)}}+O(\epsilon^{3}).

Equation (
S32
) is the central relation that will be used below to derive
the effective spin-exchange dynamics between the localized and conduction electrons (Eq. (4) in the main text).

I.2
Non-Markov environment: Keldysh theory

The previous section discussed open quantum systems whose dynamics are assumed to be Markovian.
However, there are situations where such assumptions are inappropriate, where the baths do not relax fast enough compared to the relevant system and the dynamics of the baths play a role as a result.
A prominent example is a many-body system attached to a thermal bath.
In this case, the relevant system generically approaches an equilibrium state, where general properties such as the fluctuation-dissipation theorem
[
108
]
should be satisfied.
However, these relations cannot be satisfied when we neglect the non-Markovianity arising from Fermi/Bose distribution functions
[
109
]
.
As we will be interested in situations where we continuously tune from equilibrium to non-equilibrium, a formalism that includes such effects is desired.

This subsection briefly reviews the Keldysh approach that can incorporate such effects. (For further details, we refer to e.g., Refs.
[
99
,
67
,
100
]
.)
In this approach, we describe the dynamics of the density matrix
\hat{\rho}
in terms of a path integral, which enables us to use field-theoretical techniques such as perturbative diagrammatic calculations, renormalization group methods, and makes the connection to the semi-classical limit explicit.
This will prove useful for obtaining the equation of motion of observables and will be used in the analysis performed in Sec.
IV
.

I.2.1
Keldysh formalism for closed systems

Let us start by formulating the Keldysh theory for closed systems described by the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
.
The Liouville equation reads,

i\partial_{t}\hat{\rho}=[\hat{H},\hat{\rho}],

(S33)

which has a formal solution,

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(t)=\hat{U}(t,t_{0})\hat{\rho}(t_{0})\hat{U}^{-1}(t,t_{0}),

(S34)

with the time evolution operator given by,

\displaystyle\hat{U}(t,t_{0})=e^{-i\hat{H}(t-t_{0})}.

(S35)

We set the initial state to be a thermal state at temperature
T
,

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(t=t_{0})=\frac{1}{Z_{0}}\sum_{n}e^{-E_{n}/(k_{\rm B}T)}\lvert n\rangle\langle n\rvert.

(S36)

Here,
E_{n}
is an eigenenergy,
\lvert n\rangle
is the eigenstate, and
Z_{0}=\sum_{n}e^{-E_{n}/(k_{\rm B}T)}
.
We define the Keldysh partition function as,

\displaystyle Z\equiv{\rm tr}\hat{\rho}(t_{f})={\rm tr}[\hat{U}(t_{f},t_{0})\hat{\rho}(t_{0})\hat{U}^{-1}(t_{f},t_{0})].

(S37)

Although this quantity itself is trivially computed as unity
Z=1
, the functional integral form of this quantity serves as a generating function that gives us useful macroscopic information we will be interested in.

To illustrate how this formalism works,
we will focus on a simple, single-mode fermion system
\hat{H}=\varepsilon_{0}\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}\hat{\psi}
, where
\hat{\psi}
is a fermionic annihilation operator.
Similarly to the path integral formalism in quantum mechanics, we expand the time evolution operator into a product of infinitesimally short time interval
\delta_{t}=(t_{f}-t_{0})/N
(with
N\rightarrow\infty
) and replace the annihilation operators
\hat{\psi}_{j}
(
j
labels discretized time
t_{j}=j\delta_{t}
) to a Grassmann variable in the fermionic case
\psi_{i}
with coherent states

\displaystyle\hat{\psi}_{j}\lvert\psi_{j}\rangle=\psi_{j}\lvert\psi_{j}\rangle,\qquad\langle\psi_{j}\rvert\hat{\psi}_{j}^{\dagger}=\langle\psi_{j}\rvert\psi_{j}^{*}.

(S38)

by inserting the completeness relation

\displaystyle\hat{1}=\int d\psi_{j}d\psi_{j}^{*}e^{-\psi_{j}^{*}\psi_{j}}\lvert\psi_{j}\rangle\langle\psi_{j}\rvert.

(S39)

We obtain,

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Z_{0}^{-1}\int\prod_{n}d\bar{\psi}_{+,n}d\psi_{+,n}d\bar{\psi}_{-,n}d\psi_{-,n}

(S40)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle e^{\sum_{j}[\bar{\psi}_{j,+}(\psi_{j-1,+}-\psi_{j,+}-i\delta_{t}\varepsilon_{0}\psi_{j-1,+})+\bar{\psi}_{j,-}(\psi_{j-1,-}-\psi_{j,-}+i\delta_{t}\varepsilon_{0}\psi_{j-1,-})]}e^{-\bar{\psi}_{1,+}\psi_{1,+}-\bar{\psi}_{1,-}\psi_{1,-}+\zeta\bar{\psi}_{1,+}\psi_{N,-}+\bar{\psi}_{1,-}\psi_{N,+}}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int{\mathcal{D}}[\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-}]e^{iS[\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-}]},

where we have used

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{+,n+1}|\psi_{+,n}\rangle=e^{\bar{\psi}_{+,n+1}\psi_{+,n}},\qquad\langle-\psi_{-,n}|-\psi_{-,n+1}\rangle=e^{\bar{\psi}_{-,n}\psi_{-,n+1}}

(S41)

and
Z_{0}^{-1}=\langle\psi_{+}(t_{0})\rvert\hat{\rho}(t_{0})\lvert-\psi_{-}(t_{0})\rangle
.
Here, the action has the form

S=\bar{\psi}G^{-1}\psi
, where
we have introduced a set of coherent states for both the time evolution of ket (
+
) and bra (
-
), collectively denoted as

\displaystyle\psi=\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{1,+}&\cdots&\psi_{N,+}&\psi_{1,-}&\cdots&\psi_{N,-}\end{pmatrix}^{\rm T},\qquad\bar{\psi}=\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{1,+}&\cdots&\psi_{N,+}&\psi_{1,-}&\cdots&\psi_{N,-}\end{pmatrix}

(S42)

with
\zeta=-e^{-\varepsilon_{0}/(k_{\rm B}T)}

and the kernel is given by,

\displaystyle G^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}(G^{-1})_{++}&(G^{-1})_{+-}\\
(G^{-1})_{-+}&(G^{-1})_{--}\end{pmatrix}

(S43)

with

\displaystyle(G^{-1})_{++}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0&\cdots&0\\
-a_{+}&1&0&\cdots&0\\
0&-a_{+}&1&\cdots&0\\
0&0&\ddots&\ddots&0\\
0&0&0&-a_{+}&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad(G^{-1})_{--}=i\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0&\cdots&0\\
-a_{-}&1&0&\cdots&0\\
0&-a_{-}&1&\cdots&0\\
0&0&\ddots&\ddots&0\\
0&0&0&-a_{-}&1\end{pmatrix},

(S44)

\displaystyle(G^{-1})_{+-}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&\cdots&-\zeta\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&\ddots&\ddots&0\\
0&0&0&0&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad(G^{-1})_{-+}=i\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&\cdots&1\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&\ddots&\ddots&0\\
0&0&0&0&0\end{pmatrix},

(S45)

where
a_{\pm}=1\mp i\varepsilon_{0}\delta_{t}
.
The action in the continuous-time limit
N\rightarrow\infty
reads,

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dtdt^{\prime}\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\psi}_{+}(t)&\bar{\psi}_{-}(t)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}G_{++}(t,t^{\prime})&G_{+-}(t,t^{\prime})\\
G_{-+}(t,t^{\prime})&G_{--}(t,t^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{+}(t^{\prime})\\
\psi_{-}(t^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}

(S46)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dt\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\psi}_{+}(t)&\bar{\psi}_{-}(t)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}i\partial_{t}-\varepsilon_{0}&0\\
0&-(i\partial_{t}-\varepsilon_{0})\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{+}(t)\\
\psi_{-}(t)\end{pmatrix}.

Taking the inverse of
G^{-1}
, one obtains in the continuous-time limit
N\rightarrow\infty
(see e.g. Ref.
[
99
]
for derivation),

\displaystyle G_{+-}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=if(\varepsilon_{0})e^{-i\varepsilon_{0}(t-t^{\prime})},

(S47a)

\displaystyle G_{-+}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=i(1+f(\varepsilon_{0}))e^{-i\varepsilon_{0}(t-t^{\prime})},

(S47b)

\displaystyle G_{++}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\theta(-t)G_{+-}(t,t^{\prime})+\theta(t)G_{-+}(t,t^{\prime}),

(S47c)

\displaystyle G_{--}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\theta(t)G_{+-}(t,t^{\prime})+\theta(-t)G_{-+}(t,t^{\prime}).

(S47d)

where
f(\omega)=1/(e^{\omega/(k_{\rm B}T)}+1)
is the Fermi distribution function and
\theta(t)
is a step function.
Their Fourier transformation is given by,

\displaystyle G_{+-}(\omega)

\displaystyle=2\pi i\delta(\omega-\varepsilon_{0})f(\varepsilon_{0}),

(S48a)

\displaystyle G_{-+}(\omega)

\displaystyle=-2\pi i\delta(\omega-\varepsilon_{0})(1-f(\varepsilon_{0})),

(S48b)

\displaystyle G_{++}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-f(\varepsilon_{0})}{\omega-\varepsilon_{0}+i\delta}+\frac{f(\varepsilon_{0})}{\omega-\varepsilon_{0}-i\delta},

(S48c)

\displaystyle G_{--}(\omega)

\displaystyle=-\frac{f(\varepsilon_{0})}{\omega-\varepsilon_{0}+i\delta}-\frac{1-f(\varepsilon_{0})}{\omega-\varepsilon_{0}-i\delta}.

(S48d)

These are related to the various two-point functions as
[
99
,
67
,
100
]
,

\displaystyle G_{+-}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\left<\psi_{+}(t)\bar{\psi}_{-}(t^{\prime})\right>=-i\int{\mathcal{D}}(\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-})\psi_{+}(t)\bar{\psi}_{-}(t^{\prime})e^{iS}=i\left<\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})\hat{\psi}(t)\right>

(S49)

\displaystyle G_{-+}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\left<\psi_{-}(t)\bar{\psi}_{+}(t^{\prime})\right>=-i\int{\mathcal{D}}(\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-})\psi_{-}(t)\bar{\psi}_{+}(t^{\prime})e^{iS}=-i\left<\hat{\psi}(t)\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})\right>

(S50)

\displaystyle G_{++}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\left<\psi_{+}(t)\bar{\psi}_{+}(t^{\prime})\right>=-i\int{\mathcal{D}}(\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-})\psi_{+}(t)\bar{\psi}_{+}(t^{\prime})e^{iS}=-i\left<{\mathcal{T}}\hat{\psi}(t)\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})\right>

(S51)

\displaystyle G_{--}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\left<\psi_{-}(t)\bar{\psi}_{-}(t^{\prime})\right>=-i\int{\mathcal{D}}(\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-})\psi_{-}(t)\bar{\psi}_{-}(t^{\prime})e^{iS}=-i\left<\bar{\mathcal{T}}\hat{\psi}(t)\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})\right>

(S52)

where
{\mathcal{T}}
and
\bar{\mathcal{T}}
are the time-ordered and anti-time-ordered operators, respectively.

It is often useful to change the variables via a “Wick rotation”,

\displaystyle\psi_{c}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\psi_{+}+\psi_{-}),\qquad\psi_{q}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\psi_{+}-\psi_{-}),

(S53)

to transform the action into,

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dtdt^{\prime}\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\psi}_{q}(t)&\bar{\psi}_{c}(t)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}G_{qq}(t,t^{\prime})&G_{qc}(t,t^{\prime})\\
G_{cq}(t,t^{\prime})&G_{cc}(t,t^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{q}(t^{\prime})\\
\psi_{c}(t^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}

(S54)

where the kernel in this representation is related to the correlation functions and response functions as,

\displaystyle G^{\rm K}(t,t^{\prime})\equiv G_{cc}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-i\left<\psi_{c}(t)\bar{\psi}_{c}(t^{\prime})\right>=-i\int{\mathcal{D}}(\psi_{q},\bar{\psi}_{q},\psi_{c},\bar{\psi}_{c})\psi_{c}(t)\bar{\psi}_{c}(t^{\prime})e^{iS}=-i\left<[\hat{\psi}(t),\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})]\right>

(S55a)

\displaystyle G^{\rm R}(t,t^{\prime})\equiv G_{cq}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-i\left<\psi_{c}(t)\bar{\psi}_{q}(t^{\prime})\right>=-i\int{\mathcal{D}}(\psi_{q},\bar{\psi}_{q},\psi_{c},\bar{\psi}_{c})\psi_{c}(t)\bar{\psi}_{q}(t^{\prime})e^{iS}=-i\theta(t-t^{\prime})\left<\{\hat{\psi}(t),\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})\}\right>

(S55b)

\displaystyle G^{\rm A}(t,t^{\prime})\equiv G_{qc}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-i\left<\psi_{q}(t)\bar{\psi}_{c}(t^{\prime})\right>=-i\int{\mathcal{D}}(\psi_{q},\bar{\psi}_{q},\psi_{c},\bar{\psi}_{c})\psi_{q}(t)\bar{\psi}_{c}(t^{\prime})e^{iS}=i\theta(t^{\prime}-t)\left<\{\hat{\psi}(t),\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}(t^{\prime})\}\right>

(S55c)

\displaystyle 0=G_{qq}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-i\left<\psi_{q}(t)\bar{\psi}_{q}(t^{\prime})\right>=-i\int{\mathcal{D}}(\psi_{q},\bar{\psi}_{q},\psi_{c},\bar{\psi}_{c})\psi_{q}(t)\bar{\psi}_{q}(t^{\prime})e^{iS}.

(S55d)

Here,

G^{\rm R},G^{\rm A}
, and
G^{\rm K}
are called the retarded, advanced, and Keldysh Green’s functions, respectively.
Their Fourier transform is given by,

\displaystyle G^{\rm R}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\omega-\varepsilon_{0}+i\delta},

(S56a)

\displaystyle G^{\rm A}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\omega-\varepsilon_{0}-i\delta},

(S56b)

\displaystyle G^{\rm K}(\omega)

\displaystyle=-\pi i(1-2f(\omega))\delta(\omega-\varepsilon_{0}).

(S56c)

Physically,
G^{\rm R}(\omega)[=G^{\rm A*}(\omega)]
describes the response to an external field, while
G^{\rm K}(\omega)
describes the correlation function.
One can check that the fluctuation-dissipation relation for fermions
[
108
]

\displaystyle G^{\rm K}(\omega)=(1-2f(\omega))[G^{\rm R}(\omega)-G^{\rm A}(\omega)]

(S57)

is satisfied, as it should in general closed equilibrium systems.

It is worth pointing out that the action Eq. (
S46
) (or Eq. (
S54
)) vanishes when one puts
\psi_{+}=\psi_{-}
(or equivalently,
\psi_{q}=0
).
This so-called causality structure originates from the trace-preserving property of the governing dynamics
[
99
,
67
,
100
]
.
This can be seen by noting that, replacing
\psi_{+}
to
\psi_{-}
implies moving the time evolution operator
\hat{U}(t_{f},t_{0})
that time-evolves the ket in the Keldysh partition function (
S37
) to the bra-dynamics,
using the cyclic property of the trace.

It is straightforward to generalize this result to many-body systems.
An example relevant to our study is a free electron gas described by the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}=\sum_{\bm{k}}\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{\bm{k}}\hat{c}_{\bm{k}}
.
The action is given by,

\displaystyle S=\int dtdt^{\prime}\sum_{\bm{k}}\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\psi}_{\bm{k}}^{+}(t)&\bar{\psi}_{\bm{k}}^{-}(t)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}G_{\bm{k}}^{++}(t,t^{\prime})&G_{\bm{k}}^{+-}(t,t^{\prime})\\
G_{\bm{k}}^{-+}(t,t^{\prime})&G_{\bm{k}}^{--}(t,t^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{\bm{k}}^{+}(t^{\prime})\\
\psi_{\bm{k}}^{-}(t^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}

(S58)

where

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}G_{0}^{++}(\bm{k},\omega)&G_{0}^{+-}(\bm{k},\omega)\\
G_{0}^{-+}(\bm{k},\omega)&G_{0}^{--}(\bm{k},\omega)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\big[\frac{1-f_{\bm{k}}}{\omega-\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}+i\delta}+\frac{f_{\bm{k}}}{\omega-\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}-i\delta}\big]&2\pi i\delta(\omega-\varepsilon_{\bm{k}})f_{\bm{k}}\\
-2\pi i\delta(\omega-\varepsilon_{\bm{k}})(1-f_{\bm{k}})&\big[-\frac{f_{\bm{k}}}{\omega-\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}+i\delta}-\frac{1-f_{\bm{k}}}{\omega-\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}-i\delta}\big]\end{pmatrix}.

(S59)

I.2.2
Open quantum system attached to a thermal bath

Keldysh formalism is useful for formulating open quantum systems.
To illustrate this, we will show an example of a single-mode fermion attached to a thermal bath modeled by a collection of free fermions.
The Hamiltonian
\hat{H}=\hat{H}_{s}+\hat{H}_{b}+\hat{H}_{sb}
is given by,

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{s}=\varepsilon_{0}\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}\psi,\qquad\hat{H}_{b}=\sum_{\mu}\omega_{\mu}\hat{\phi}_{\mu}^{\dagger}\phi_{\mu},\qquad\hat{H}_{sb}=\sum_{\mu}[v_{\mu}\hat{\phi}_{\mu}^{\dagger}\hat{\psi}+{\rm h.c.}],

(S60)

where
\hat{\phi}_{\mu}
and
\omega_{\mu}
are the fermionic annihilation operator and the energy of bath fermions at mode
\mu
, respectively.
The Keldysh partition function can be obtained for this system as,

\displaystyle Z=\int{\mathcal{D}}[\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-},\phi_{+},\bar{\phi}_{+},\phi_{-},\bar{\phi}_{-}]e^{iS_{\rm tot}[\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-},\phi_{+},\bar{\phi}_{+},\phi_{-},\bar{\phi}_{-}]},

(S61)

where the total action is composed of three parts
S_{\rm tot}=S_{0}+S_{b}+S_{sb}
, which are given by,

\displaystyle S_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dtdt^{\prime}\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\psi}_{+}(t)&\bar{\psi}_{-}(t)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}G_{++}(t,t^{\prime})&G_{+-}(t,t^{\prime})\\
G_{-+}(t,t^{\prime})&G_{--}(t,t^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{+}(t^{\prime})\\
\psi_{-}(t^{\prime})\end{pmatrix},

(S62)

\displaystyle S_{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu}\int dtdt^{\prime}\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\phi}_{+}^{\mu}(t)&\bar{\phi}_{-}^{\mu}(t)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}B_{++}^{\mu}(t,t^{\prime})&B_{+-}^{\mu}(t,t^{\prime})\\
B_{-+}^{\mu}(t,t^{\prime})&B_{--}^{\mu}(t,t^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{+}^{\mu}(t^{\prime})\\
\phi_{-}^{\mu}(t^{\prime})\end{pmatrix},

(S63)

\displaystyle S_{sb}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu}\int dt[(v_{\mu}\bar{\phi}_{+}(t)\psi_{+}(t)+{\rm c.c.})-(v_{\mu}\bar{\phi}_{-}(t)\psi_{-}(t)+{\rm c.c.})].

(S64)

Here,
\phi_{\pm},\bar{\phi}_{\pm}
are the Grassmann variables of the baths,

G_{s,s^{\prime}}(s,s^{\prime}=+,-)
is given by Eq. (
S47
), and

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}B_{++}^{\mu}(t,t^{\prime})&B_{+-}^{\mu}(t,t^{\prime})\\
B_{-+}^{\mu}(t,t^{\prime})&B_{--}^{\mu}(t,t^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}i\theta(-t)f(\omega_{\mu})e^{-i\varepsilon_{0}(t-t^{\prime})}+i\theta(t)(1+f(\omega_{\mu}))e^{-i\omega_{\mu}(t-t^{\prime})}&if(\omega_{\mu})e^{-i\omega_{\mu}(t-t^{\prime})}\\
i(1+f(\omega_{\mu}))e^{-i\omega_{\mu}(t-t^{\prime})}&i\theta(t)f(\omega_{\mu})e^{-i\omega_{\mu}(t-t^{\prime})}+i\theta(-t)(1+f(\omega_{\mu}))e^{-i\omega_{\mu}(t-t^{\prime})}\end{pmatrix}

are the various two-point correlation functions of the baths.

Since the total action
S_{\rm tot}
is quadratic in terms of the bath Grassmann variables
\phi_{\pm}
, one can analytically integrate out the bath degrees of freedom as,

\displaystyle Z=\int{\mathcal{D}}[\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-}]e^{iS_{\rm eff}[\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-}]},

(S66)

where

\displaystyle e^{iS_{\rm eff}[\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-}]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{iS_{0}[\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-}]}\int{\mathcal{D}}[\phi_{+},\bar{\phi}_{+},\phi_{-},\bar{\phi}_{-}]e^{iS_{b}[\phi_{+},\bar{\phi}_{+},\phi_{-},\bar{\phi}_{-}]+S_{sb}[\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-},\phi_{+},\bar{\phi}_{+},\phi_{-},\bar{\phi}_{-}]}

(S67)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{iS_{0}[\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-}]}e^{i\Delta S[\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-}]}.

The contribution arising from the attachment of the bath
\Delta S
is computed as,

\displaystyle\Delta S[\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu}|v_{\mu}|^{2}\int dtdt^{\prime}\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\psi}_{+}(t)&\bar{\psi}_{-}(t)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}B_{++}^{\mu}(t,t^{\prime})&B_{+-}^{\mu}(t,t^{\prime})\\
B_{-+}^{\mu}(t,t^{\prime})&B_{--}^{\mu}(t,t^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{+}(t^{\prime})\\
\psi_{-}(t^{\prime})\end{pmatrix},

(S68)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu}|v_{\mu}|^{2}\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\psi}_{q}(\omega)&\bar{\psi}_{c}(\omega)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_{\mu}-i\delta}\\
\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_{\mu}+i\delta}&-\pi i(1-2f(\omega))\delta(\omega-\omega_{\mu})\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{q}(\omega)\\
\psi_{c}(\omega)\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\psi}_{q}(\omega)&\bar{\psi}_{c}(\omega)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&i\frac{\gamma(\omega)}{2}\\
-i\frac{\gamma(\omega)}{2}&i\frac{\gamma(\omega)}{2}(1-2f(\omega))\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{q}(\omega)\\
\psi_{c}(\omega)\end{pmatrix}

Here, in the second equality, we have moved to the quantum-classical representation Eq. (
S53
).
In the third, we have ignored the contribution from the real part of the propagators (the “Lamb shift”) and have introduced a dissipation rate
\gamma(\omega)=\pi\sum_{\mu}|v_{\mu}|^{2}\delta(\omega-\omega_{\mu})
,
where we have taken the spectrum of the bath fermions to be a continuum.

The effective action
S_{\rm eff}=S_{0}+\Delta S
is therefore summarized as,

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dtdt^{\prime}\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\psi}_{q}(\omega)&\bar{\psi}_{c}(\omega)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&G^{\rm A}(\omega)\\
G^{\rm R}(\omega)&G^{\rm K}(\omega)\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{q}(\omega)\\
\psi_{c}(\omega)\end{pmatrix},

(S69)

where the correlation functions and response functions are given by,

\displaystyle G^{\rm R}(\omega)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\omega-\varepsilon_{0}+i\gamma(\omega)},

(S70)

\displaystyle G^{\rm A}(\omega)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\omega-\varepsilon_{0}-i\gamma(\omega)},

(S71)

\displaystyle G^{\rm K}(\omega)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\pi(1-2f(\omega))\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{\gamma(\omega)}{(\omega-\varepsilon_{0})^{2}+\gamma^{2}(\omega)}.

(S72)

Compared with the closed system counterpart (Eq. (
S56
)),
the delta-functions of the spectrum in Eq. (
S56
) is replaced by a Lorentzian. This shows that the coupling to the bath gives rise to dissipation in the relevant system.

Remarkably, the fluctuation-dissipation theorem (Eq. (
S57
)) is still satisfied with this attachment to the thermal bath.
This illustrates how the Keldysh theory rightly describes how an attachment to a thermal bath thermalizes the relevant system.
(In fact, this can be understood as a result of a certain symmetry of the Keldysh action that is respected in the thermal equilibrium, see Ref.
[
109
]
.)
We remark that this could not have been captured in the GKSL formalism discussed in Sec.
I.1

[
109
]
, where the Markovian approximation ignores the dynamical effects arising from the Fermi distribution function.

I.2.3
Keldysh technique applied to the GKSL master equation

So far, we have considered the case where the relevant system is attached to a single bath, reproducing the results known from the equilibrium statistical mechanics.
The real advantage of using the Keldysh approach is that it can treat nonequilibrium systems arising from attaching to multiple baths.

In our study, we will be concerned with a system (localized electrons) attached to two baths.
One of the baths is Markovian (the higher-energy state)
and the other is non-Markovian (conduction electrons).
As we have reviewed in Sec.
I.1
,
once we integrated out the Markovian bath,
the reduced density operator
\hat{\rho}
is governed by
the GKSL equation (
S4
), reproduced below for convenience:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\hat{\rho}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}\hat{\rho}=-i[\hat{H},\hat{\rho}]+\sum_{\ell}\kappa_{\ell}{\mathcal{D}}[\hat{L}_{\ell}]\hat{\rho},

(S73)

with
{\mathcal{D}}[\hat{L}]\hat{\rho}=\hat{L}\hat{\rho}\hat{L}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{L}^{\dagger}\hat{L},\hat{\rho}\}
.
Therefore, we will have in mind the situation where the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
is composed of the relevant system and the non-Markovian bath (but the formalism below is general).

Following Refs.
[
67
,
100
]
,
we formulate a Keldysh path integral formalism for the GKSL equation to treat this type of open system.
As done in the previous subsections,
we define the Keldysh action

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm tr}[\hat{\rho}(t_{f})]={\rm tr}\big[e^{{\mathcal{L}}(t_{f}-t_{0})}\hat{\rho}(t_{0})\big]={\rm tr}\Big[\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\prod_{n=0}^{N}\hat{\rho}_{n}\Big]

(S74)

where we have discretized the GKSL equation (
S73
) as

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{n+1}=(\hat{1}+\delta_{t}{\mathcal{L}})[\hat{\rho}_{n}]+O(\delta_{t}^{2}).

(S75)

with
\delta_{t}=(t_{f}-t_{0})/N
and defined
\hat{\rho}_{0}\equiv\hat{\rho}(t_{0})
.

Let us restrict ourselves, for simplicity, to a single-mode fermion system.
As before, we expand the Keldysh partition function into coherent states.
This can be computed once
\langle\psi_{+,n+1}\rvert\hat{\rho}_{n+1}\lvert-\psi_{-,n+1}\rangle=\langle\psi_{+,n+1}\rvert(\hat{1}+\delta_{t}{\mathcal{L}})[\hat{\rho}_{n}]\lvert-\psi_{-,n+1}\rangle
is calculated.
Using

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{+,n+1}\rvert{\mathcal{L}}\hat{\rho}_{n}\lvert-\psi_{-,n+1}\rangle

(S76)

\displaystyle=\int d\bar{\psi}_{+,n}d\psi_{+,n}d\bar{\psi}_{-,n}d\psi_{-,n}e^{-\bar{\psi}_{+,n}\psi_{+,n}-\bar{\psi}_{-,n}\psi_{-,n}}\langle\psi_{+,n}\rvert\hat{\rho}_{n}\lvert-\psi_{-,n+1}\rangle\langle\psi_{+,n+1}\rvert{\mathcal{L}}[\lvert\psi_{+,n}\rangle\langle-\psi_{-,n}\rvert]\lvert-\psi_{-,n+1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int d\bar{\psi}_{+,n}d\psi_{+,n}d\bar{\psi}_{-,n}d\psi_{-,n}e^{-\bar{\psi}_{+,n}\psi_{+,n}-\bar{\psi}_{-,n}\psi_{-,n}}\langle\psi_{+,n}\rvert\hat{\rho}_{n}\lvert-\psi_{-,n}\rangle

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\Bigg[(-i)\Big[\langle\psi_{+,n+1}\rvert\hat{H}\lvert\psi_{+,n}\rangle\langle-\psi_{-,n}|-\psi_{-,n+1}\rangle-\langle\psi_{+,n+1}|\psi_{+,n}\rangle\langle-\psi_{-,n}\rvert\hat{H}\lvert-\psi_{-,n+1}\rangle\Big]

\displaystyle+\sum_{\ell}\kappa_{\ell}\Big[\langle\psi_{+,n+1}\rvert\hat{L}_{\ell}\lvert\psi_{+,n}\rangle\langle-\psi_{-,n}\rvert\hat{L}_{\ell}^{\dagger}\lvert-\psi_{-,n+1}\rangle

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ -\frac{1}{2}\Big[\langle\psi_{+,n+1}\rvert\hat{L}_{\ell}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{\ell}\lvert\psi_{+,n}\rangle\langle-\psi_{-,n}|-\psi_{-,n+1}\rangle+\langle\psi_{+,n+1}|\psi_{+,n}\rangle\langle-\psi_{-,n}\rvert\hat{L}_{\ell}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{\ell}\lvert-\psi_{-,n+1}\rangle\Big]\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\int d\bar{\psi}_{+,n}d\psi_{+,n}d\bar{\psi}_{-,n}d\psi_{-,n}e^{-(\bar{\psi}_{+,n}-\bar{\psi}_{+,n+1})\psi_{+,n}-\bar{\psi}_{-,n}(\psi_{-,n}-\psi_{-,n+1})}\langle\psi_{+,n}\rvert\hat{\rho}_{n}\lvert-\psi_{-,n}\rangle

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\Bigg[(-i)\Big[H_{+}(\psi_{+,n},\bar{\psi}_{+,n})-H_{-}(\psi_{-,n},\bar{\psi}_{-,n})\Big]+\sum_{\ell}\kappa_{\ell}\Big[L_{+,n,\ell}(-\bar{L}_{-,n,\ell})-\frac{1}{2}\big(\bar{L}_{+,n,\ell}L_{+,n,\ell}+\bar{L}_{-,n,\ell}L_{-,n,\ell}\big)\Big]\Bigg]

where

\displaystyle H_{+}(\psi_{+,n},\bar{\psi}_{+,n})=\langle\psi_{+,n}\rvert\hat{H}\lvert\psi_{+,n}\rangle,\quad H_{-}(\psi_{-,n},\bar{\psi}_{-,n})=\langle-\psi_{-,n}\rvert\hat{H}\lvert-\psi_{-,n}\rangle,

(S77)

\displaystyle L_{+,n,\ell}=\langle\psi_{+,n}\rvert\hat{L}_{\ell}\lvert\psi_{+,n}\rangle,\qquad L_{-,n,\ell}=\langle-\psi_{-,n}\rvert\hat{L}_{\ell}\lvert-\psi_{-,n}\rangle,

(S78)

This yields,

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{+,n+1}\rvert\hat{\rho}_{n+1}\lvert-\psi_{-,n+1}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\bar{\psi}_{+,n}d\psi_{+,n}d\bar{\psi}_{-,n}d\psi_{-,n}e^{-(\bar{\psi}_{+,n}-\bar{\psi}_{+,n+1})\psi_{+,n}-\bar{\psi}_{-,n}(\psi_{-,n}-\psi_{-,n+1})}e^{\delta_{t}{\mathcal{L}}[\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-}]}

with

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}[\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-i)\Big[H_{+}(\psi_{+,n},\bar{\psi}_{+,n})-H_{-}(\psi_{-,n},\bar{\psi}_{-,n})\Big]

(S80)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell}\kappa_{\ell}\Big[L_{+,n,\ell}(-\bar{L}_{-,n,\ell})-\frac{1}{2}\big(\bar{L}_{+,n,\ell}L_{+,n,\ell}+\bar{L}_{-,n,\ell}L_{-,n,\ell}\big)\Big]

Here, we have assumed
that the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
, the dissipators
\hat{L}_{\ell}
, and
\hat{L}_{\ell}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{\ell}
are normal ordered.
We note that there are subtleties in this normal ordering procedure, where the causality structure may be broken
[
110
]
.
This can be mitigated by introducing an infinitesimal time difference that enforces the ordering of the operators, see e.g. Appendix A in Ref.
[
100
]
for details.
Taking the continuous limit,
the Keldysh partition function
can be computed as

\displaystyle Z=\int{\mathcal{D}}[\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-}]e^{iS[\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-}]},

(S81)

with the Keldysh action
S
given by,

\displaystyle S[\psi_{+},\bar{\psi}_{+},\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{-}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dt\Big[\bar{\psi}_{+}(t)(i\partial_{t}-H_{+}(t))\psi_{+}(t)-\bar{\psi}_{-}(t)(i\partial_{t}-H_{-}(t))\psi_{+}(t)

(S82)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell}\kappa_{\ell}\big[L_{+,\ell}(t)\bar{L}_{-,\ell}(t)-\frac{1}{2}(\bar{L}_{+,\ell}(t_{+\delta})L_{+,\ell}(t_{-\delta})+\bar{L}_{-,\ell}(t_{-\delta})L_{-,\ell}(t_{+\delta}))\big]\Big]

Here, we have introduced
t_{\pm\delta}=t\pm 0^{+}
to ensure the causality structure of the action, i.e.,
S[\psi_{+}=\psi_{-},\bar{\psi}_{+}=\bar{\psi}_{-}]=0
.
Equation (
S82
) that gives the relation between the GKSL equation (
S73
) and the Keldysh action
S
is the central relation used in Sec.
IV
.

II
The model

Equipped with the theoretical tools introduced in Sec.
I
, we formulate below our dissipation-engineering scheme to control local spin-spin interactions in magnetic metals.
This section summarizes our model.

As illustrated schematically in Fig. 2(a) in the main text, this system is composed of localized electrons immersed in a Fermi sea of conduction electrons.
The localized electrons are responsible for magnetic properties, while the conduction electrons are responsible for metallic properties.
To manipulate the effective spin-spin interactions between the localized sites (Fig. 2(b) in the main text), we inject a laser light that gives rise to dissipation to the double-occupied state at site
a
at the rate
\kappa_{a}
.
By tuning the frequency of the laser
h\nu
to be resonant with a higher-level state (say, upper-level
f
-orbital state) that has a linewidth
\Gamma_{f,a}
, (Fig. 2(c) in the main text),
we turn on tunneling from the double-occupied state to the higher-level state (
a
labels the localized electron sites).

This process can be regarded as a Markov process, as long as the dissipation rate of the higher-level state
\Gamma_{f,a}
(which is the timescale of the memory of the bath) is larger than its supply rate.
A detailed discussion on this point is provided in Methods.
As explained in Sec.
I.1
, the time evolution of reduced system density operator
\hat{\rho}
of such Markov system is generally governed by the GKSL master equation.
In our system, it reads

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\hat{\rho}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}\hat{\rho}

(S83)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i[\hat{H},\hat{\rho}]+\sum_{a,\sigma}\kappa_{a}{\mathcal{D}}[\hat{d}_{\sigma,a}\hat{P}_{\uparrow\downarrow}^{a}]\hat{\rho}+\sum_{a,\sigma}\delta_{a}{\mathcal{D}}[\hat{d}_{\sigma,a}^{\dagger}\hat{P}_{\varnothing}^{a}]\hat{\rho}.

The first term describes the coherent dynamics of the magnetic metals and the second term is the light-induced dissipation.
We have added the third term phenomenologically to ensure the steady state of localized electrons is singly occupied at each site.
(We have omitted the third term in the main text, as they only play a role in ensuring the state is in the singly occupied state.)

The magnetic metal is modeled by the Anderson impurity model
\hat{H}=\hat{H}_{0}+\hat{H}_{cd}
consisting of two parts.
The unperturbed part
\hat{H}_{0}=\hat{H}_{c0}+\hat{H}_{d0}
describes the conduction electrons and the localized electrons, given respectively by,

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{c0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{k},\sigma=\uparrow,\downarrow}\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{\bm{k}\sigma}\hat{c}_{\bm{k}\sigma},

(S84)

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{d0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a}\Big[\sum_{\sigma=\uparrow,\downarrow}\varepsilon_{d,a}\hat{d}^{\dagger}_{\sigma,a}\hat{d}_{\sigma,a}+U_{a}\hat{d}^{\dagger}_{\uparrow,a}\hat{d}_{\uparrow,a}\hat{d}^{\dagger}_{\downarrow,a}\hat{d}_{\downarrow,a}\Big].

(S85)

Here, the conduction electrons are modeled as free electrons, where
\hat{c}_{\bm{k},\sigma}
is a fermionic annihilation operator of conduction electrons with momentum
\bm{k}
and spin
\sigma=\uparrow,\downarrow
and
\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}
is the kinetic energy.
We assume that the conduction electrons’ system size is large enough to ensure they are always in thermal equilibrium.
Its distribution obeys the Fermi distribution function
f(\varepsilon_{\bm{k}})=[e^{(\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}-\varepsilon_{\rm F})/(k_{\rm B}T)}+1]^{-1}
that is characterized by temperature
T
and the Fermi energy
\varepsilon_{\rm F}
.
We will focus on low-temperature regime
k_{\rm B}T\ll\varepsilon_{\rm F}
.
The localized electrons are modeled as a two-level system with energy
\varepsilon_{d,a}
and an on-site Coulomb repulsion
U_{a}>0
at site
a
.
Here,
\hat{d}_{\sigma,a}
is a fermionic annihilation operator of localized electrons at site
a
.
These two types of electrons couple through the second part of the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
, namely the c-d mixing
\hat{H}_{cd}
, where the electrons in the conduction band tunnel at rate
v_{a}
to the localized orbital and vice versa, described by

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{cd}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a,\sigma}\big[v_{a}\hat{d}_{\sigma,a}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{\bm{R}_{a}\sigma}+{\rm h.c.}\big]=\sum_{a}\sum_{\bm{k},\sigma}\big[v_{a}e^{i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{R}_{a}}\hat{d}_{\sigma,a}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{\bm{k}\sigma}+{\rm h.c.}\big],

(S86)

Here,
\hat{c}_{\bm{r},\sigma}=\sum_{\bm{k}}e^{i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{r}}\hat{c}_{\bm{k},\sigma}
is the Fourier transform of
\hat{c}_{\bm{k},\sigma}
and
\bm{R}_{a}
is the position of the local electron at site
a
.
In what follows, we restrict our interest to systems where the Coulomb repulsion is strong enough (
U_{a}\gg v_{a}
) to treat
\hat{H}_{cd}
as a perturbation.

The second term in the GKSL master equation (
S83
)
describes the light-induced loss of an electron in the double-occupied state.
The localized electron at site
a
can take four states: it can be vacant
\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{a}
, singly occupied with
\uparrow(\downarrow)
-spin
\lvert\uparrow(\downarrow)\rangle_{a}=\hat{d}_{a,\uparrow(\downarrow)}^{\dagger}\lvert\varnothing\rangle
, or double-occupied occupied
\lvert\uparrow\downarrow\rangle_{a}=\hat{d}_{a,\uparrow}^{\dagger}\hat{d}_{a,\downarrow}^{\dagger}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{a}
.

\hat{P}_{\uparrow\downarrow}^{a}
at site
a
projects the state to the double-occupied state
\lvert\uparrow\downarrow\rangle_{a}
,
enforcing the loss to activate only when site
a
is double-occupied.
It will become clear in the following that this selective decay of localized electrons effectively modifies the interactions between the localized spins.

The third term
(
\delta_{a}\rightarrow 0^{+}
) is phenomenologically added to drive the system towards a singly occupied state (
\lvert\uparrow\rangle_{a}
or
\lvert\downarrow\rangle_{a}
), where
\hat{P}_{\varnothing}^{a}
is the projection operator to the vacant state
\lvert\varnothing\rangle
at site
a
.

We note that the GKSL equation (
S83
) (which serves as our starting point) can be derived microscopically from a Hamiltonian directly modeling the laser injection as a time-dependent drive that couples the double-occupied state and a higher order state. This is detailed in Sec.
V
.

III
Effective localized spin dynamics immersed in conduction electrons with light-induced correlated dissipation

To proceed, we resort to the projection method introduced in Sec.
I.1.2
to perturbatively derive the effective low-energy dynamics given by Eq. (4) in the main text.
We split the Lindbladian
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}_{0}+{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
in Eq. (
S83
) to non-perturbative (
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
) and perturbative part (
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
) as follows:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\hat{\rho}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i[\hat{H}_{c0}+\hat{H}_{d0},\hat{\rho}]+\sum_{a,\sigma}\kappa_{a}{\mathcal{D}}[\hat{d}_{\sigma,a}\hat{P}_{\uparrow\downarrow}^{a}]\hat{\rho}+\sum_{a,\sigma}\delta_{a}{\mathcal{D}}[\hat{d}_{\sigma,a}^{\dagger}\hat{P}_{\varnothing}^{a}]\hat{\rho},

(S87)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i[\hat{H}_{cd},\hat{\rho}].

(S88)

The perturbative part
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
, i.e. the c-d mixing, gives a contribution at most
O(v_{a}/U_{a})
, which is assumed to be small.
In this section,
we will use the relation Eq. (
S32
) to derive the low-energy effective Lindbladian of our system (Eq. (
S83
)) within the second-order perturbation in
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
, which we reproduce below for convenience:

\displaystyle({\mathcal{L}}_{\rm eff})_{n_{l},n_{r}}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle(\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)},{\mathcal{L}_{\rm eff}\hat{r}_{n_{r}}^{(0)}})={\rm tr}[\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)\dagger}{\mathcal{L}_{\rm eff}\hat{r}_{n_{r}}^{(0)}}]

(S89)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm tr}[\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)\dagger}{\mathcal{L}_{0}\hat{r}_{n_{r}}^{(0)}}]+{\rm tr}[\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)\dagger}{\mathcal{L}_{1}\hat{r}_{n_{r}}^{(0)}}]-\sum_{m\in\mathfrak{f}}\frac{{\rm tr}[\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)\dagger}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\hat{r}_{m}^{(0)}]{\rm tr}[\hat{l}_{m}^{(0)\dagger}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\hat{r}_{n_{r}}^{(0)}]}{\lambda_{m}^{(0)}}+O(({\mathcal{L}}_{1})^{3}).

Here,
\hat{r}_{n_{r}}^{(0)}
and
\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)}
are the right and left eigenstates, respectively, that form a basis of the slow variable space we define below.

(\hat{A},\hat{B})={\rm tr}[\hat{A}^{\dagger}\hat{B}]
is the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product (see Eq. (
S8
)).
In the third term, the sum is taken over the fast degrees of freedom, also defined below.

III.1
Characterization of non-perturbative Lindbladian
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}

As is clear from the expression of Eq. (
S89
), the first step
to derive the effective low-energy Lindbladian
is to characterize the (left) right eigenstates
\hat{r}_{n}^{(0)}(\hat{l}_{n}^{(0)})
and eigenvalues of
the unperturbed Lindbladian
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
(Eq. (
S87
)).
This can be done exactly since the non-perturbative Lindbladian

{\mathcal{L}}_{0}={\mathcal{L}}_{c0}+\sum_{a}{\mathcal{L}}_{d0,a}
is composed of free conduction electrons and localized electrons that are completely decoupled, where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{c0}\hat{\rho}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\big[\sum_{\bm{k},\sigma}\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}\hat{c}_{\bm{k},\sigma}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{\bm{k},\sigma},\hat{\rho}\big],

(S90)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{d0,a}\hat{\rho}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\big[\big(\sum_{\sigma}\varepsilon_{d,a}\hat{d}^{\dagger}_{\sigma,a}\hat{d}_{\sigma,a}+U_{a}\hat{d}^{\dagger}_{\uparrow,a}\hat{d}_{\uparrow,a}\hat{d}^{\dagger}_{\downarrow,a}\hat{d}_{\downarrow,a}\big),\hat{\rho}]+\sum_{\sigma}\kappa_{a}{\mathcal{D}}[\hat{d}_{\sigma,a}\hat{P}_{\uparrow\downarrow}^{a}]\hat{\rho}+\sum_{\sigma}\delta_{a}{\mathcal{D}}[\hat{d}_{\sigma,a}^{\dagger}\hat{P}_{\varnothing}^{a}]\hat{\rho}.

(S91)

The eigenvectors can be expressed as a direct product,
i.e.
\hat{r}_{n}^{(0)}=\hat{r}_{n}^{c(0)}\otimes\prod_{a}\hat{r}_{a,n}^{d(0)}
and

\hat{l}_{n}^{(0)}=\hat{l}_{n}^{c(0)}\otimes\prod_{a}\hat{l}_{a,n}^{d(0)}
.
Here,
\hat{r}_{n}^{c(0)}
and
\hat{l}_{n}^{c(0)}
are the right and left eigenstates, respectively, of the (non-perturbative) conduction electron Lindbladian
{\mathcal{L}}_{c0}
that satisfies,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{c0}\hat{r}_{n}^{c(0)}=\lambda_{n}^{c(0)}\hat{r}_{n}^{c(0)},\qquad{\mathcal{L}}_{c0}^{\dagger}\hat{l}_{n}^{c(0)}=\lambda_{n}^{c(0)*}\hat{l}_{n}^{c(0)},

(S92)

with an eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}^{c(0)}
.
Similarly,
\hat{r}_{a,n}^{d(0)}
and
\hat{l}_{a,n}^{d(0)}
are the right and left eigenstates, respectively, with an eigenvalue
\lambda_{a,n}^{d(0)}
for the localized electron part
{\mathcal{L}}_{d0,a}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{d0,a}\hat{r}_{a,n}^{d(0)}=\lambda_{a,n}^{d(0)}\hat{r}_{a,n}^{d(0)},\qquad{\mathcal{L}}_{d0,a}^{\dagger}\hat{l}_{a,n}^{d(0)}=\lambda_{a,n}^{d(0)*}\hat{l}_{a,n}^{d(0)}.

(S93)

The total eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}^{(0)}
is given by their sum
\lambda_{n}^{(0)}=\lambda_{n}^{c(0)}+\sum_{a}\lambda_{a,n}^{d(0)}
.

III.1.1
Characterization of
{\mathcal{L}_{d0,a}}

Let us first characterize
{\mathcal{L}_{d0,a}}
.
It is helpful to point out that the superoperator

{\mathcal{N}_{a,\sigma}^{d}}\hat{\rho}=[\hat{d}_{\sigma,a}^{\dagger}\hat{d}_{\sigma,a},\hat{\rho}]
commutes with
{\mathcal{L}_{d0,a}}
,
implying that the two superoperators share a set of eigenvectors.
The eigenvector of the superoperator
{\mathcal{N}_{a,\sigma}^{d}}
is the outer product of Fock states with corresponding eigenvalue
m_{\uparrow}(m_{\downarrow})=0,\pm 1
.
Here, the eigenvalue
m_{\uparrow}(m_{\downarrow})=0,\pm 1
is given by the number of
\sigma=\uparrow(\downarrow)
-spin electrons in ket minus those in bra.
Therefore, the right and left eigenvectors of

{\mathcal{L}_{d0,a}}
can be cast into a block-diagonal form

{\mathcal{L}_{d0,a}}=\otimes_{m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow}}{\mathcal{L}_{d0,a,(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})}}
, where
m_{\sigma}
is the eigenvalue of
{\mathcal{N}}_{a,\sigma}^{d}
.
The simultaneous right and left eigenstates of
{\mathcal{L}_{d0,a}}
and
{\mathcal{N}}_{a,\sigma}^{d}
are given respectively by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{d0,a,(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})}\hat{r}_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})}^{d(0)}=\lambda_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})}^{d(0)}\hat{r}_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})}^{d(0)},\qquad{\mathcal{L}}_{d0,a,(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})}^{\dagger}\hat{l}_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})}^{d(0)}=\lambda_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})}^{d(0)*}\hat{l}_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})}^{d(0)}.

(S94)

For example,
(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})=(0,0)
sector has the right and left eigenmode of the form

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{a,\ell,m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0}^{d(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0),\varnothing}^{(0)}\lvert\varnothing\rangle\langle\varnothing\rvert+r_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0),\uparrow}^{(0)}\lvert\uparrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\rvert

(S95)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle r_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0),\downarrow}^{(0)}\lvert\downarrow\rangle\langle\downarrow\rvert+r_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0),\uparrow\downarrow}^{(0)}\lvert\uparrow\downarrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\downarrow\rvert,

\displaystyle\hat{l}_{a,\ell,m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0}^{d(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle l_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0),\varnothing}^{(0)}\lvert\varnothing\rangle\langle\varnothing\rvert+l_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0),\uparrow}^{(0)}\lvert\uparrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\rvert

(S96)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle l_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0),\downarrow}^{(0)}\lvert\downarrow\rangle\langle\downarrow\rvert+l_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0),\uparrow\downarrow}^{(0)}\lvert\uparrow\downarrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\downarrow\rvert.

Similarly, the right and left eigenvectors have the form

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=1,m_{\downarrow}=0)}^{d(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=1,m_{\downarrow}=0),\varnothing}^{(0)}\lvert\uparrow\rangle\langle\varnothing\rvert+r_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=1,m_{\downarrow}=0),\downarrow}^{(0)}\lvert\uparrow\downarrow\rangle\langle\downarrow\rvert,

(S97)

\displaystyle\hat{l}_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=1,m_{\downarrow}=0)}^{d(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle l_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=1,m_{\downarrow}=0),\varnothing}^{(0)}\lvert\uparrow\rangle\langle\varnothing\rvert+l_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=1,m_{\downarrow}=0),\downarrow}^{(0)}\lvert\uparrow\downarrow\rangle\langle\downarrow\rvert,

(S98)

for
(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})=(1,0)
,

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=-1,m_{\downarrow}=0)}^{d(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=-1,m_{\downarrow}=0),\uparrow}^{(0)}\lvert\varnothing\rangle\langle\uparrow\rvert+r_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=-1,m_{\downarrow}=0),\uparrow\downarrow}^{(0)}\lvert\downarrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\downarrow\rvert,

(S99)

\displaystyle\hat{l}_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=-1,m_{\downarrow}=0)}^{d(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle l_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=-1,m_{\downarrow}=0),\uparrow}^{(0)}\lvert\varnothing\rangle\langle\uparrow\rvert+l_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=-1,m_{\downarrow}=0),\uparrow\downarrow}^{(0)}\lvert\downarrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\downarrow\rvert,

(S100)

for
(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})=(-1,0)
, and so on.

Let us start with the analysis of the eigenvectors of
(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})=(0,0)
-sector, which is often called the
T_{1}
-modes that physically correspond to the population dynamics of the states.
In this sector, one finds by plugging the form Eqs. (
S95
) and (
S96
) into Eqs. (
S94
),

\displaystyle\bm{M}_{a}\bm{r}_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0)}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0)}^{(0)}\bm{r}_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0)}^{(0)},\qquad\bm{r}_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0)}^{(0)}=\begin{pmatrix}r_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0),\varnothing}^{(0)}\\
r_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0),\uparrow}^{(0)}\\
r_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0),\downarrow}^{(0)}\\
r_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0),\uparrow\downarrow}^{(0)}\end{pmatrix},

(S101)

\displaystyle(\bm{l}_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0)}^{(0)})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{M}_{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0)}^{(0)}(\bm{l}_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0)}^{(0)})^{\mathsf{T}},\qquad\bm{l}_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0)}^{(0)}=\begin{pmatrix}l_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0),\varnothing}^{(0)}\\
l_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0),\uparrow}^{(0)}\\
l_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0),\downarrow}^{(0)}\\
l_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=0,m_{\downarrow}=0),\uparrow\downarrow}^{(0)}\end{pmatrix},

(S102)

with

\displaystyle\bm{M}_{a}=\begin{pmatrix}-2\delta_{a}&0&0&0\\
\delta_{a}&0&0&\kappa_{a}\\
\delta_{a}&0&0&\kappa_{a}\\
0&0&0&-2\kappa_{a}\end{pmatrix}.

(S103)

Note that the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
gives no contribution to this Fock-space diagonal subspace, where the dynamical matrix
\bm{M}_{a}
contains no contribution from
\hat{H}
.
This is due to the property that
\hat{H}
is diagonal in the Fock space.

The first four rows of Table
1
report the eigenvalues and the right and left eigenvectors in the
(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})=(0,0)
sector, computed by diagonalizing
\bm{M}_{a}
.
Note that the first two eigenvectors are degenerate with zero eigenvalues.
This is because both spin configurations of singly occupied state (
\lvert\uparrow\rangle
and
\lvert\downarrow\rangle
) do not experience any dissipation in
{\mathcal{L}}_{d0,a}
.
We also note that this zero-eigenvalue degenerate space of the left eigenvectors contains the identity operator
\hat{I}
, as is expected from the general result (Eq. (
S23
)).

We now move on to the analysis of other sectors
(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})\neq(0,0)
(often called the
T_{2}
-modes).
Noting that the jump term
\hat{d}_{\sigma,a}\hat{P}_{\uparrow\downarrow}\hat{r}_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})}\hat{P}_{\uparrow\downarrow}\hat{d}_{\sigma,a}^{\dagger}=0~((m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})\neq(0,0))

does not contribute in this sector because of the presence of the projection operator
\hat{P}_{\uparrow\downarrow}
,
the eigenvalues and eigenstates are given as a simple beamsplitter operator, see Table
1
.
This can be readily checked.
For example, for the
(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})=(1,0)
sector,
{\mathcal{L}}_{d0}\hat{r}_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=1,m_{\downarrow}=0)}
can be compute as,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{d0}(r_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=1,m_{\downarrow}=0),\varnothing}^{(0)}\lvert\uparrow\rangle\langle\varnothing\rvert+r_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=1,m_{\downarrow}=0),\downarrow}^{(0)}\lvert\uparrow\downarrow\rangle\langle\downarrow\rvert)

\displaystyle=\Big(-i\varepsilon_{d,a}+\frac{\delta_{a}}{2}\Big)r_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=1,m_{\downarrow}=0),\varnothing}^{(0)}\lvert\uparrow\rangle\langle\varnothing\rvert+\Big(-i(\varepsilon_{d,a}+U_{a})-\frac{\kappa_{a}}{2}\Big)r_{a,\ell,(m_{\uparrow}=1,m_{\downarrow}=0),\downarrow}^{(0)}\lvert\uparrow\downarrow\rangle\langle\downarrow\rvert

(S104)

which is diagonal, giving the eigenvalues and eigenstates listed in the seventh and ninth row of Table
1
.

As mentioned earlier, while the
T_{1}
-modes (the
(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})=(0,0)
sector) only involve dissipation, the
T_{2}
-modes (the
(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})\neq(0,0)
sector) involves coherence between the two different states.
The oscillation frequencies of the
T_{2}
-modes are the difference between the energies of two states, which are of order
O(\varepsilon_{d,a},U_{a})
.
We regard the first six (the last six) eigenvalues on Table
1
as the slow (fast) modes.
We will perturbatively project out the fast modes to obtain the effective dynamics including the effect of the c-d mixing
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
.

Table 1:
Eigenvalues and eigenstates of
{\mathcal{L}}_{d0,a}

Eigenvalue
\lambda_{a,n}^{d(0)}

Right eigenstate
\hat{r}_{a,n}^{d(0)}

Left eigenstate
\hat{l}_{a,n}^{d(0)}

slow

0

\frac{1}{2}[\lvert\uparrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\rvert+\lvert\downarrow\rangle\langle\downarrow\rvert]

\hat{I}

0

\frac{1}{2}[\lvert\uparrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\rvert-\lvert\downarrow\rangle\langle\downarrow\rvert]

\frac{1}{2}[\lvert\uparrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\rvert-\lvert\downarrow\rangle\langle\downarrow\rvert]

-2\delta_{a}

\lvert\uparrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\rvert+\lvert\downarrow\rangle\langle\downarrow\rvert-2\lvert\varnothing\rangle\langle\varnothing\rvert

-\frac{1}{2}\lvert\varnothing\rangle\langle\varnothing\rvert

-2\kappa_{a}

\lvert\uparrow\downarrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\downarrow\rvert-\frac{1}{2}\lvert\uparrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\rvert-\frac{1}{2}\lvert\downarrow\rangle\langle\downarrow\rvert

\lvert\uparrow\downarrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\downarrow\rvert

0

\lvert\uparrow\rangle\langle\downarrow\rvert

\lvert\uparrow\rangle\langle\downarrow\rvert

0

\lvert\downarrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\rvert

\lvert\downarrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\rvert

fast

-i\varepsilon_{d,a}-\frac{\delta_{a}}{2}

\lvert\sigma\rangle\langle\varnothing\rvert

\lvert\sigma\rangle\langle\varnothing\rvert

i\varepsilon_{d,a}-\frac{\delta_{a}}{2}

\lvert\varnothing\rangle\langle\sigma\rvert

\lvert\varnothing\rangle\langle\sigma\rvert

-i(\varepsilon_{d,a}+U_{a})-\frac{\kappa_{a}}{2}

\lvert\uparrow\downarrow\rangle\langle\sigma\rvert

\lvert\uparrow\downarrow\rangle\langle\sigma\rvert

i(\varepsilon_{d,a}+U_{a})-\frac{\kappa_{a}}{2}

\lvert\sigma\rangle\langle\uparrow\downarrow\rvert

\lvert\sigma\rangle\langle\uparrow\downarrow\rvert

-i(2\varepsilon_{d,a}+U_{a})-\frac{\kappa_{a}}{2}

\lvert\uparrow\downarrow\rangle\langle\varnothing\rvert

\lvert\uparrow\downarrow\rangle\langle\varnothing\rvert

i(2\varepsilon_{d,a}+U_{a})-\frac{\kappa_{a}}{2}

\lvert\varnothing\rangle\langle\uparrow\downarrow\rvert

\lvert\varnothing\rangle\langle\uparrow\downarrow\rvert

III.1.2
Characterization of
{\mathcal{L}_{c0}}

Let us next analyze
{\mathcal{L}}_{c0}
.
As noted earlier, we assume that the system size of the conduction electrons is large enough such that they are always in the equilibrium state.
Assuming further that the system is at low temperature
k_{\rm B}T\ll\varepsilon_{\rm F}
, the steady state (
{\mathcal{L}}_{c0}\hat{\rho}_{c,ss}^{(0)}=0
) is approximately given by a Fermi sphere
\hat{\rho}_{c,ss}^{(0)}=\lvert F\rangle\langle F\rvert
, where

\displaystyle\lvert F\rangle=\prod_{\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}<\varepsilon_{\rm F}}\prod_{\sigma=\uparrow,\downarrow}\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{\bm{k},\sigma}\lvert 0\rangle,

(S105)

and
\lvert 0\rangle
is a vacuum state.
Since
{\mathcal{L}}_{c0}
is composed of free electrons, one can obtain all the eigenvalues and eigenstates by adding or subtracting electrons from this state to both ket and bra space.
For example, by creating or annihilating an electron above or below the Fermi sphere, given respectively by,

\displaystyle\lvert\bm{k}\sigma\rangle\equiv\hat{c}_{\bm{k},\sigma}^{\dagger}\lvert F\rangle~(\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}>\varepsilon_{\rm F}),\qquad\lvert\overline{\bm{k}\sigma}\rangle\equiv\hat{c}_{\bm{k},\sigma}\lvert F\rangle~(\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}<\varepsilon_{\rm F})

(S106)

then

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{c0}(\lvert\bm{k},\sigma\rangle\langle F\rvert)

\displaystyle=-i\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}(\lvert\bm{k}\sigma\rangle\langle F\rvert),

(S107a)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{c0}(\lvert F\rangle\langle\bm{k},\sigma\rvert)

\displaystyle=+i\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}(\lvert F\rangle\langle\bm{k},\sigma\rvert),

(S107b)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{c0}(\lvert\overline{\bm{k}\sigma}\rangle\langle F\rvert)

\displaystyle=+i\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}(\lvert\overline{\bm{k}\sigma}\rangle\langle F\rvert),

(S107c)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{c0}(\lvert F\rangle\langle\overline{\bm{k}\sigma}\rvert)

\displaystyle=-i\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}(\lvert F\rangle\langle\overline{\bm{k}\sigma}\rvert),

(S107d)

gives a set of eigenvalues and right eigenstates of
{\mathcal{L}}_{c0}
.
The left eigenstate is identical to the right eigenstate, which is immediate from the property
{\mathcal{L}}_{c0}^{\dagger}={\mathcal{L}}_{c0}^{*}
.
Similarly, by further adding one more particle or a hole to these states, one obtains further sets of eigenvalues and right eigenstates of
{\mathcal{L}}_{c0}
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{c0}(\lvert\bm{k}\sigma,\overline{\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}\rangle\langle F\rvert)

\displaystyle=-i(\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}-\varepsilon_{\bm{k}^{\prime}})(\lvert\bm{k}\sigma,\overline{\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}\rangle\langle F\rvert),

(S108a)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{c0}(\lvert F\rangle\langle\bm{k},\sigma,\overline{\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}\rvert)

\displaystyle=+i(\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}-\varepsilon_{\bm{k}^{\prime}})(\lvert F\rangle\langle\bm{k},\sigma,\overline{\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}\rvert),

(S108b)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{c0}(\lvert\bm{k}\sigma\rangle\langle\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}\rvert)

\displaystyle=-i(\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}-\varepsilon_{\bm{k}^{\prime}})(\lvert\bm{k}\sigma\rangle\langle\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}\rvert),

(S108c)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{c0}(\lvert\overline{\bm{k}\sigma}\rangle\langle\overline{\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}\rvert)

\displaystyle=+i(\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}-\varepsilon_{\bm{k}^{\prime}})(\lvert\overline{\bm{k}\sigma}\rangle\langle\overline{\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}\rvert),

(S108d)

etc., where

\displaystyle\lvert\bm{k}\sigma,\overline{\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}\rangle\equiv\hat{c}_{\bm{k}\sigma}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}\lvert F\rangle~(\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}>\varepsilon_{\rm F},\varepsilon_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}<\varepsilon_{\rm F}).

(S109)

One can continue the same procedure to obtain all sets of eigenvalues and eigenstates.

Note crucially that, at the low-temperature regime we are interested in, the energy of the created/removed electrons from the Fermi sphere are generically near the Fermi surface, i.e.,
\varepsilon_{\bm{k}},\varepsilon_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}\sim\varepsilon_{\rm F}
.
Therefore, the eigenvalues of the eigenstates with different total charges of excitation in the ket and bra space (such as those in Eq. (
S107
)) are at least
O(\varepsilon_{\rm F})
, while those with the same charge in ket and bra space (such as those in Eq. (
S108
)) are generically small.
We will regard the latter (former) as the slow (fast) modes in the next subsection.

III.2
Derivation of Eq. (4) in the main text

Having derived the right and left eigenstates of the non-perturbative Lindbladian
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
,
we are now in the position to derive the effective low-energy master equation (Eq. (4) in the main text)
that incorporates the effect of c-d mixing
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
within the second-order perturbation.
In what follows, we will explicitly compute the matrix element of the low-energy effective Lindbladian
({\mathcal{L}}_{\rm eff,sd})_{n_{l},n_{r}}=(\hat{l}_{n_{l}},{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm eff,sd}\hat{r}_{n_{r}}^{(0)})={\rm tr}[\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)\dagger}{\mathcal{L}}_{\rm eff,sd}\hat{r}_{n_{r}}^{(0)}]
(see Eq. (
S89
)).

We will mainly be interested in the singly-occupied case, where we take the right eigenstate
\hat{r}_{n_{r}}^{(0)}

as a singly occupied state for the localized electrons and a Fermi sphere for the conduction electron,
given by (
\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-}=\uparrow,\downarrow
)

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{n_{r}}^{(0)}=\hat{r}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\sigma_{-};F)}^{(0)}=\lvert\sigma_{+}\rangle\lvert F\rangle\langle\sigma_{-}\rvert\langle F\rvert.

(S110)

This state has a zero eigenvalue in the unperturbed Lindbladian, i.e.,
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\hat{r}^{(0)}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\sigma_{-};F)}=0
.
For simplicity, we have temporarily dropped the localized electron label
a
and will focus on a single impurity problem (as the extension to the multiple impurity case is straightforward).
We will discuss the matrix elements that involves
T_{1}
modes with vacant
\lvert\varnothing\rangle\langle\varnothing\rvert
and double occupied state
\lvert\uparrow\downarrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\downarrow\rvert
later.
The left eigenstate
\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)}
is taken from the slow variable space defined in the previous section, see Table
1
and the final paragraph of Sec.
III.1.2
.
One can readily check that the zeroth order contribution in Eq. (
S89
) vanishes, i.e.,

({\mathcal{L}}_{\rm eff,sd})_{n_{l},[(\sigma_{+};F),(\sigma_{-};F)]}^{(0)}={\rm tr}[\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)\dagger}{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\hat{r}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\sigma_{-};F)}^{(0)}]=0
.

For later convenience, we find it useful to introduce the c-d mixing operators,

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{d\Leftarrow c}^{a\bm{k}\sigma}=v_{a}e^{i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{R}_{a}}\hat{d}_{\sigma,a}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{\bm{k}\sigma},\qquad\hat{V}_{c\Leftarrow d}^{a\bm{k}\sigma}=v_{a}^{*}e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{R}_{a}}\hat{c}_{\bm{k}\sigma}^{\dagger}\hat{d}_{\sigma,a}(=(\hat{V}_{d\Leftarrow c}^{a\bm{k}\sigma})^{\dagger}),

(S111)

that simplifies the notation of perturbative Lindbladian to

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}^{(1)}\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i[\hat{H}_{cd},\hat{\rho}]=-i\sum_{a}\sum_{\bm{k},\sigma}\big[\hat{V}_{d\Leftarrow c}^{a\bm{k}\sigma}+\hat{V}_{c\Leftarrow d}^{a\bm{k}\sigma},\hat{\rho}\big].

(S112)

The state
\hat{r}^{(0)}_{\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-};F}
is perturbed with
{\mathcal{L}}^{(1)}
as,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}^{(1)}\hat{r}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\sigma_{-};F)}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\sum_{\bm{k},\sigma}\big[(\hat{V}_{d\Leftarrow c}^{a\bm{k}\sigma}+\hat{V}_{c\Leftarrow d}^{a\bm{k}\sigma})(\lvert\sigma_{+}\rangle\lvert F\rangle\langle\sigma_{-}\rvert\langle F\rvert)-(\lvert\sigma_{+}\rangle\lvert F\rangle\langle\sigma_{-}\rvert\langle F\rvert)(\hat{V}_{d\Leftarrow c}^{a\bm{k}\sigma\dagger}+\hat{V}_{c\Leftarrow d}^{a\bm{k}\sigma\dagger})\big]

(S113)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\sum_{\bm{k},\sigma}\Big[\big[v_{a}e^{i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{R}_{a}}\delta_{\sigma_{+},-\sigma}\delta_{\bm{k}\in F}(\lvert\uparrow\downarrow\rangle\lvert\overline{\bm{k}\sigma}\rangle\langle\sigma_{-}\rvert\langle F\rvert)+v_{a}^{*}e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{R}_{a}}\delta_{\sigma_{+},\sigma}\delta_{\bm{k}\in\overline{F}}(\lvert\varnothing\rangle\lvert\bm{k}\sigma\rangle\langle\sigma_{-}\rvert\langle F\rvert)\big]

\displaystyle\ \ -\big[(\lvert\sigma_{+}\rangle\lvert F\rangle\langle\uparrow\downarrow\rvert\langle\overline{\bm{k}\sigma}\rvert)v_{a}^{*}e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{R}_{a}}\delta_{\sigma_{-},-\sigma}\delta_{\bm{k}\in F}+(\lvert\sigma_{+}\rangle\lvert F\rangle\langle\varnothing\rvert\langle\bm{k}\sigma\rvert)v_{a}e^{i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{R}_{a}}\delta_{\sigma_{-},\sigma}\delta_{\bm{k}\in\overline{F}}\big]\Big]

where we have introduced

\delta_{\bm{k}\in F}=1(0)
when
\bm{k}\in F
is an (un)occupied state, for simplicity of notation.
We have also introduced the notation
\uparrow\equiv-\downarrow
and
\downarrow\equiv-\uparrow
, again for simplicity of notation.
Notice how the c-d mixing
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
transfers the state in the slow variable space to the fast variable state.
The localized electron state is transferred from a singly occupied state in the
(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})=(0,0)
,
(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})=(1,-1)
or
(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})=(-1,1)
sector
to a different sector, i.e.,

(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})=(\pm 1,0)
,
(m_{\uparrow},m_{\downarrow})=(0,\pm 1)
sector.
This property immediately tells us that the first-order contribution in Eq. (
S89
) vanishes, i.e.,
({\mathcal{L}}_{\rm eff,sd})_{n_{l},[(\sigma_{+};F),(\sigma_{-};F)]}^{(1)}={\rm tr}[\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)\dagger}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\hat{r}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\sigma_{-};F)}^{(0)}]=0
.

We, therefore, concentrate below on the second-order contribution in Eq. (
S89
),

\displaystyle({\mathcal{L}}_{\rm eff,sd})_{n_{l},[(\sigma_{+};F),(\sigma_{-};F)]}^{(2)}=-\sum_{m\in\mathfrak{f}}[\lambda_{m}^{(0)}]^{-1}(\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)},{\mathcal{L}}^{(1)}\hat{r}_{m}^{(0)})(\hat{l}_{m}^{(0)},{\mathcal{L}}^{(1)}\hat{r}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\sigma_{-};F)}^{(0)}),

(S114)

where the sum is taken over the fast modes defined in Secs.
III.1.1
and
III.1.2
.
A straightforward calculation using the results derived in Sec.
III.1.1
and
III.1.2
yields,

\displaystyle({\mathcal{L}}_{\rm eff,sd})_{n_{l},[(\sigma_{+};F),(\sigma_{-};F)]}^{(2)}=\sum_{\bm{k},\bm{k}^{\prime},\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\times\Bigg[\frac{(\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)},\hat{V}^{a\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}_{c\Leftarrow d}\hat{r}_{(\uparrow\downarrow;(\overline{\bm{k},-\sigma_{+}})),(\sigma_{-};F)}^{(0)}-\hat{r}_{(\uparrow\downarrow;(\overline{\bm{k},-\sigma_{+}})),(\sigma_{-};F)}^{(0)}\hat{V}^{a\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}\dagger}_{d\Leftarrow c})(\hat{l}_{(\uparrow\downarrow;(\overline{\bm{k},-\sigma_{+}})),(\sigma_{-};F)}^{(0)},\hat{V}^{a\bm{k}\sigma}_{d\Leftarrow c}\hat{r}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\sigma_{-};F)}^{(0)})}{-i(\varepsilon_{d,a}+U_{a}-\varepsilon_{\bm{k}})-\frac{\kappa_{a}}{2}}

\displaystyle-\frac{(\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)},\hat{V}^{a\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}_{d\Leftarrow c}\hat{r}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\uparrow\downarrow;(\overline{\bm{k},-\sigma_{-}}))}^{(0)}-\hat{r}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\uparrow\downarrow;(\overline{\bm{k},-\sigma_{-}}))}^{(0)}\hat{V}^{a\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}\dagger}_{c\Leftarrow d})(\hat{l}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\uparrow\downarrow;(\overline{\bm{k},-\sigma_{-}}))}^{(0)},\hat{r}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\sigma_{-};F)}^{(0)}\hat{V}^{a\bm{k}\sigma\dagger}_{d\Leftarrow c})}{i(\varepsilon_{d,a}+U_{a}-\varepsilon_{\bm{k}})-\frac{\kappa_{a}}{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{(\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)},\hat{V}^{a\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}_{d\Leftarrow c}\hat{r}_{(\varnothing;(\bm{k},\sigma_{+})),(\sigma_{-};F)}^{(0)}-\hat{r}_{(\varnothing;(\bm{k},\sigma_{+})),(\sigma_{-};F)}^{(0)}\hat{V}^{a\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}\dagger}_{c\Leftarrow d})(\hat{l}_{(\varnothing;(\bm{k},\sigma_{+})),(\sigma_{-};F)}^{(0)},\hat{V}^{a\bm{k}\sigma}_{c\Leftarrow d}\hat{r}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\sigma_{-};F)}^{(0)})}{-i(\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}-\varepsilon_{d,a})-\frac{\delta_{a}}{2}}

\displaystyle-\frac{(\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)},\hat{V}^{a\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}_{c\Leftarrow d}\hat{r}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\varnothing;(\bm{k},\sigma_{-}))}^{(0)}-\hat{r}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\varnothing;(\bm{k},\sigma_{-}))}^{(0)}\hat{V}^{a\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}\dagger}_{d\Leftarrow c})(\hat{l}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\varnothing;(\bm{k},\sigma_{-}))}^{(0)},\hat{r}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\sigma_{-};F)}^{(0)}\hat{V}^{a\bm{k}\sigma\dagger}_{c\Leftarrow d})}{i(\varepsilon_{\bm{k}}-\varepsilon_{d,a})-\frac{\delta_{a}}{2}}\Bigg].

(S115)

Here, we have introduced the notation for the intermediate states,

\displaystyle\hat{r}^{(0)}_{(\uparrow\downarrow;(\overline{\bm{k},-\sigma_{+}})),(\sigma_{-};F)}=\hat{l}^{(0)}_{(\uparrow\downarrow;(\overline{\bm{k},-\sigma_{+}})),(\sigma_{-};F)}

\displaystyle=\lvert\uparrow\downarrow\rangle\lvert\overline{\bm{k},-\sigma_{+}}\rangle\langle\sigma_{-}\rvert\langle F\rvert,

(S116a)

\displaystyle\hat{r}^{(0)}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\uparrow\downarrow;(\overline{\bm{k},-\sigma_{-}}))}=\hat{l}^{(0)}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\uparrow\downarrow;(\overline{\bm{k},-\sigma_{-}}))}

\displaystyle=\lvert\sigma_{+}\rangle\lvert F\rangle\langle\uparrow\downarrow\rvert\langle\overline{\bm{k},-\sigma_{-}}\rvert,

(S116b)

\displaystyle\hat{r}^{(0)}_{(\varnothing;(\bm{k},\sigma_{+})),(\sigma_{-};F)}=\hat{l}^{(0)}_{(\varnothing;(\bm{k},\sigma_{+})),(\sigma_{-};F)}

\displaystyle=\lvert\varnothing\rangle\lvert\bm{k},\sigma_{+}\rangle\langle\sigma_{-}\rvert\langle F\rvert,

(S116c)

\displaystyle\hat{r}^{(0)}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\varnothing;(\bm{k},-\sigma_{-}))}=\hat{l}^{(0)}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\varnothing;(\bm{k},-\sigma_{-})}

\displaystyle=\lvert\sigma_{+}\rangle\lvert F\rangle\langle\varnothing\rvert\langle\bm{k},-\sigma_{-}\rvert.

(S116d)

Let us focus first on the first two terms that involve a double-occupied state as their intermediate state, which we denote
({\mathcal{L}}_{\rm eff,sd})^{(2{\rm A})}_{n_{l},[(\sigma_{+};F),(\sigma_{-};F)]}
:

\displaystyle({\mathcal{L}}_{\rm eff,sd})^{(2{\rm A})}_{n_{l},[(\sigma_{+};F),(\sigma_{-};F)]}=\sum_{\bm{k},\bm{k}^{\prime},\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\times\Bigg[\frac{(\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)},\hat{V}^{a\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}_{c\Leftarrow d}\hat{r}_{(\uparrow\downarrow;(\overline{\bm{k},-\sigma_{+}})),(\sigma_{-};F)}^{(0)}-\hat{r}_{(\uparrow\downarrow;(\overline{\bm{k},-\sigma_{+}})),(\sigma_{-};F)}^{(0)}\hat{V}^{a\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}\dagger}_{d\Leftarrow c})(\hat{l}_{(\uparrow\downarrow;(\overline{\bm{k},-\sigma_{+}})),(\sigma_{-};F)}^{(0)},\hat{V}^{a\bm{k}\sigma}_{d\Leftarrow c}\hat{r}_{\sigma_{-};F}^{(0)})}{-i(\varepsilon_{d,a}+U_{a}-\varepsilon_{\bm{k}})-\frac{\kappa_{a}}{2}}

\displaystyle-\frac{(\hat{l}_{n_{l}}^{(0)},\hat{V}^{a\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}}_{d\Leftarrow c}\hat{r}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\uparrow\downarrow;(\overline{\bm{k},-\sigma_{-}}))}^{(0)}-\hat{r}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\uparrow\downarrow;(\overline{\bm{k},-\sigma_{-}}))}^{(0)}\hat{V}^{a\bm{k}^{\prime}\sigma^{\prime}\dagger}_{c\Leftarrow d})(\hat{l}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\uparrow\downarrow;(\overline{\bm{k},-\sigma_{-}}))}^{(0)},\hat{r}_{(\sigma_{+};F),(\sigma_{-};F)}^{(0)}\hat{V}^{a\bm{k}\sigma\dagger}_{d\Leftarrow c})}{i(\varepsilon_{d,a}+U_{a}-\varepsilon_{\bm{k}})-\frac{\kappa_{a}}{2}}\Bigg].

(S117)

One can proceed with the calculation as,

\displaystyle({\mathcal{L}}_{\rm eff,sd})^{(2{\rm A})}_{n_{l},[(\sigma_{+};F),(\sigma_{-};F)]}=\sum_{\bm{k},\bm{k}^{\prime},\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}
